# Manuscript Title
**GRACE: A Narrative-Centered Intelligent Community Computing Ecosystem for Elder Wellbeing with the GRACE Index, Daily Hero’s-Journey Story Creation, Fast State Prediction, and Smart Stimulus Agents**

## Running Title
**GRACE for Narrative Elder Wellbeing**

---

## Abstract
Population aging has intensified the need for digital systems that support the wellbeing of older adults. Yet many existing technologies focus primarily on safety, health monitoring, and service coordination, often overlooking meaning, identity, belonging, and contribution. This paper proposes **GRACE (Growing, Resourceful, Active, and Enriched Life)**, an intelligent community computing ecosystem for elderly wellbeing designed as a multiagent system centered on **narrative identity**. GRACE supports older adults in sustaining relationships with the spiritual self, family and friends, working colleagues, communities, and the wider world, including markets and public services. The framework conceptualizes later life as an ongoing performance within a “theater of life,” where wellbeing depends on the quality of relationships and the exchanges of contribution they enable. To operationalize this framework, we introduce the **GRACE Index**, a daily composite measure of whether a person remains “in the zone” of narrative selfhood. We further extend GRACE with three innovations: **daily hero’s-journey story creation**, an **AI-based fast state predictor** derived from GRACE Index trajectories, and a **smart stimulus agent** that initiates personalized interventions to preserve wellbeing and self-authorship. Finally, we propose an **agent-based simulation experiment** to demonstrate the effects of these innovations. Together, these contributions shift aging technologies from deficit-oriented monitoring toward predictive, narrative, and self-stabilizing ecosystems that sustain dignity, reciprocity, and life authorship during difficult and challenging times.

**Keywords:** elder wellbeing; narrative identity; community computing; multiagent systems; aging; wellbeing measurement; predictive AI; agent-based simulation

---

# 1. Introduction
The growth of aging populations is transforming healthcare systems, families, labor structures, and public institutions. In response, digital technologies for older adults have expanded rapidly, particularly in telehealth, remote monitoring, medication management, emergency response, and care coordination. While such systems address important practical needs, many of them frame aging primarily through decline, dependency, and risk.

This framing is incomplete. Older adults are not only care recipients or service users. They are also storytellers, spiritual beings, mentors, workers, family anchors, citizens, memory bearers, and continuing authors of life. Their wellbeing depends not merely on physical safety or symptom management, but on continuity of identity, meaningful relationships, opportunities to contribute, and the ability to interpret experience within an unfolding life story.

This paper proposes **GRACE (Growing, Resourceful, Active, and Enriched Life)**, an intelligent community computing ecosystem for elder wellbeing. GRACE is designed as a **multiagent system** that supports an older person in developing and maintaining relationships across five domains: **the spiritual self, family and friends, working colleagues, communities, and the wider world including markets and public services**. The system is grounded in the view that wellbeing emerges from the quality of these relationships and the exchanges of care, recognition, service, and contribution that flow through them.

A central innovation of GRACE is that it places **narrative identity** at the core of system design. GRACE is conceived as a **live autobiographer**, helping the elder remain connected to selfhood across daily life, major transitions, and adversity. It is also a **splendid theater of life** in which the elder continues to perform, interpret, and share the epic story of life.

To make this framework measurable and actionable, we introduce the **GRACE Index**, a daily narrative-centered measure that evaluates whether the person remains **“in the zone”** of narrative selfhood. We further add three innovations: (1) a **Daily Story Creation Engine** that frames each day as part of a **hero’s journey**, (2) an **AI-based Fast State Predictor** that forecasts near-future wellbeing state from GRACE Index patterns, and (3) a **Smart Stimulus Agent** that initiates personalized prompts to maintain wellbeing based on predicted state. Finally, we propose an **agent-based simulation experiment** to demonstrate these innovations and compare their impact against baseline systems.

This paper makes five contributions:
1. a narrative-centered conceptual framework for elder wellbeing;
2. a multiagent community computing architecture;
3. the GRACE Index as a daily measure of narrative selfhood;
4. three predictive and intervention-oriented innovations; and
5. a simulation-based experimental design to demonstrate their value.

---

# 2. Literature Review

## 2.1. Aging Technologies and Deficit-Oriented Design
Many technologies for older adults have focused on health, safety, monitoring, and compliance (Sixsmith & Gutman, 2013; Czaja et al., 2006; Mitzner et al., 2010). These systems are useful, but often represent elders through deficits rather than through meaning, authorship, and contribution (Vines et al., 2015). As a result, they may support survival while neglecting dignity and identity continuity.

## 2.2. Social Connectedness and Relational Wellbeing
Research consistently links social connectedness with better health, lower loneliness, and stronger resilience (Holt-Lunstad et al., 2010; Cacioppo & Hawkley, 2009; Carstensen et al., 1999). Yet meaningful connection is not simply a matter of contact frequency. It includes trust, reciprocity, recognition, belonging, and shared memory (Cornwell & Waite, 2009; Berkman et al., 2000). Systems for elder wellbeing must therefore support not just communication, but relationship quality.

## 2.3. Narrative Identity in Later Life
Narrative identity theory suggests that people understand themselves through evolving life stories (McAdams, 2001; Habermas & Bluck, 2000; Singer, 2004). In later life, narrative continuity becomes especially important as people reinterpret their past, face transitions, and construct legacy (Butler, 1963; Bohlmeijer et al., 2007). Yet most digital systems use static profiles rather than dynamic autobiographical models. A narrative-centered system better reflects personhood.

## 2.4. Generativity, Contribution, and Reciprocity
Older adults continue to contribute through mentoring, caregiving, advising, volunteering, storytelling, and wisdom-sharing (McAdams & de St. Aubin, 1992; Erikson, 1982; Baltes & Smith, 2008). Systems that frame them only as care receivers miss a major source of wellbeing: usefulness and generativity (Morrow-Howell et al., 2017). Reciprocal contribution should be central to elder-oriented computing.

## 2.5. Community Computing and Intelligent Multiagent Systems
Community computing explores digital infrastructures for social participation and local engagement (Gurstein, 2007; Carroll, 2014). Multiagent systems enable coordination, personalization, and adaptive support (Jennings et al., 1998; Wooldridge, 2009). However, these approaches have rarely been integrated around elder narrative identity. GRACE addresses this gap.

## 2.6. Research Gap
There remains limited work integrating:
- narrative identity,
- relationship quality,
- contribution exchange,
- community participation,
- service and market access, and
- daily predictive measurement of narrative selfhood.

GRACE is proposed to address this gap.

---

# 3. Conceptual Framework: GRACE

## 3.1. Definition
**GRACE** is an intelligent community computing ecosystem for elderly wellbeing. It supports relationships across five domains:
1. spiritual self,
2. family and friends,
3. working colleagues,
4. communities, and
5. the wider world, including markets and public services.

## 3.2. Core Assumption
The central assumption of GRACE is that elder wellbeing is fundamentally **relational, narrative, and contribution-based**. Older adults flourish when they remain connected to meaningful others, grounded in values, able to contribute, and capable of experiencing life as an unfolding story that is still theirs.

## 3.3. Theater of Life Metaphor
GRACE is conceived as a **theater of life**:
- the elder is the **lead performer and author**;
- life contexts are **stages**;
- events are **scenes**;
- human and AI participants are the **cast**; and
- narrative identity is the **script in progress**.

This metaphor emphasizes continued authorship rather than passive decline.

## 3.4. Living Autobiography Model
At the center of GRACE is a **Living Autobiography Model**, containing:
- life chapters,
- key relationships,
- roles and identities,
- values and beliefs,
- memories and transitions,
- strengths and skills,
- rituals and meaningful places, and
- current concerns and future hopes.

This model guides personalization across the ecosystem.

---

# 4. The GRACE Index

## 4.1. Purpose
The **GRACE Index** is a daily composite measure designed to assess whether an older adult remains **“in the zone”** of narrative selfhood. This means remaining connected to identity, meaning, dignity, belonging, contribution, and adaptive continuity even during difficult and challenging times.

The GRACE Index is not a happiness score. It is a measure of **narrative integrity**.

## 4.2. Dimensions
The index includes six dimensions:
1. **Narrative Continuity**
2. **Spiritual Grounding**
3. **Relational Vitality**
4. **Contribution and Usefulness**
5. **Community and World Engagement**
6. **Adaptive Resilience**

## 4.3. Example Formula
Let:
- \(N\): Narrative Continuity
- \(S\): Spiritual Grounding
- \(R\): Relational Vitality
- \(C\): Contribution and Usefulness
- \(E\): Community and World Engagement
- \(A\): Adaptive Resilience

Then:

\[
G_t = 0.22N_t + 0.14S_t + 0.20R_t + 0.16C_t + 0.13E_t + 0.15A_t
\]

where \(G_t\) is the GRACE Index on day \(t\).

## 4.4. Zones
- **85-100**: Flourishing Zone  
- **70-84**: Stable Narrative Zone  
- **50-69**: Vulnerability Zone  
- **30-49**: Disruption Zone  
- **0-29**: Fragmentation Risk Zone  

---

# 5. Three New Innovations

## 5.1. Innovation 1: Daily Story Creation Through the Hero’s Journey
GRACE includes a **Daily Story Creation Engine** that interprets everyday experience through the **hero’s journey**. Rather than recording events as disconnected facts, the system transforms them into narrative structure.

Possible daily elements include:
- Ordinary World
- Call to Adventure
- Trial or Obstacle
- Guide or Companion
- Act of Courage
- Gift or Insight
- Return

This enables the elder to reinterpret challenge as meaningful struggle and to preserve agency during adversity.

Let \(H_t\) denote a **heroic narrative integration score**:

\[
H_t \in [0,1]
\]

A simple model:

\[
H_t = \alpha_1T_t + \alpha_2Gd_t + \alpha_3Co_t + \alpha_4In_t
\]

where:
- \(T_t\): trial recognition
- \(Gd_t\): guide/companion presence
- \(Co_t\): courage/action taken
- \(In_t\): insight or gift extracted

This daily story integration supports narrative continuity.

## 5.2. Innovation 2: AI-Based Fast State Predictor
GRACE includes an **AI Fast State Predictor** that forecasts near-future wellbeing state from recent GRACE Index trajectories and contextual signals.

Inputs may include:
- current GRACE Index,
- 3-day and 7-day trends,
- subscale patterns,
- major life events,
- story tone and integration,
- social contact patterns, and
- contribution activity.

Prediction target:

\[
\hat{G}_{t+\tau} = P(G_{t-\ell:t}, X_{t-\ell:t}, H_{t-\ell:t}, U_{t-\ell:t})
\]

where:
- \(P\): predictor,
- \(\ell\): lookback window,
- \(\tau\): prediction horizon,
- \(X\): dimension vectors, and
- \(U\): event/context variables.

The predictor estimates risk of entering vulnerable or disrupted states before severe decline occurs.

## 5.3. Innovation 3: Smart Stimulus Agent
GRACE includes a **Smart Stimulus Agent** that initiates personalized stimuli based on predicted state. These stimuli are designed to preserve narrative selfhood rather than manipulate behavior.

Stimulus categories:
- **Narrative** prompts
- **Relational** reconnection prompts
- **Spiritual** grounding prompts
- **Contribution** invitations
- **Community** engagement suggestions
- **Practical** support prompts

Let action \(a_t\) be selected from action space \(\mathcal{A}\):

\[
a_t \in \mathcal{A} = \{a^{nar}, a^{rel}, a^{spi}, a^{con}, a^{com}, a^{prac}, a^{none}\}
\]

Policy:

\[
a_t = \pi(\hat{Z}_{t+1}, X_t, p)
\]

where:
- \(\hat{Z}_{t+1}\): predicted zone,
- \(X_t\): current state, and
- \(p\): user preferences/profile.

These actions aim to maintain or restore the person to the stable narrative zone.

---

# 6. GRACE Multiagent Architecture

## 6.1. Core Agents
GRACE includes:
- Narrative Agent
- Relationship Agent
- Wellbeing Agent
- Spiritual Companion Agent
- Family Coordination Agent
- Work and Contribution Agent
- Community Connector Agent
- Service Access Agent
- Memory and Legacy Agent
- Ethics and Safety Agent

## 6.2. New Modules
The three innovations add:
- **Daily Story Creation Engine**
- **Fast State Predictor**
- **Smart Stimulus Agent**

## 6.3. Shared Data Core
All agents interact with:
- Living Autobiography Model
- Relationship Graph
- Contribution Ledger
- GRACE Index Engine
- Hero’s Journey Story Store
- Prediction Model Store
- Consent / Privacy / Trust Manager
- Event Timeline

## 6.4. Closed-Loop Logic
1. daily events occur;
2. story engine interprets them;
3. GRACE Index is computed;
4. predictor forecasts state;
5. smart stimulus agent selects intervention; and
6. intervention affects next-day wellbeing state.

This makes GRACE reflective, predictive, and self-stabilizing.

---

# 7. Agent-Based Simulation Experiment

## 7.1. Aim
To demonstrate that:
1. daily hero’s-journey story creation,
2. fast state prediction, and
3. smart stimulus interventions

improve elder wellbeing stability compared with baseline monitoring.

## 7.2. Experimental Question
Does the full GRACE innovation model help simulated older adults spend more time in the **Stable Narrative Zone** or **Flourishing Zone**, and recover faster from adverse events?

## 7.3. Simulated Agents
- **Elder Agent**
- **Narrative Agent**
- **Predictor Agent**
- **Stimulus Agent**
- **Social Tie Agents** (family, friends, colleagues, communities)
- **Environment Agent**

## 7.4. State Representation
For elder \(i\) on day \(t\):

\[
\mathbf{x}_{i,t} = [N_{i,t}, S_{i,t}, R_{i,t}, C_{i,t}, E_{i,t}, A_{i,t}]
\]

and total index:

\[
G_{i,t} = 0.22N_{i,t} + 0.14S_{i,t} + 0.20R_{i,t} + 0.16C_{i,t} + 0.13E_{i,t} + 0.15A_{i,t}
\]

## 7.5. State Transition
\[
\mathbf{x}_{i,t+1} = f(\mathbf{x}_{i,t}, \mathbf{u}_{i,t}, H_{i,t}, \mathbf{a}_{i,t}, \epsilon_{i,t})
\]

where:
- \(\mathbf{u}_{i,t}\): life events,
- \(H_{i,t}\): story integration,
- \(\mathbf{a}_{i,t}\): intervention action, and
- \(\epsilon_{i,t}\): noise.

## 7.6. Conditions
1. **Baseline**: monitoring only  
2. **Story Only**: daily hero’s-journey story creation  
3. **Story + Prediction**: story engine plus predictor  
4. **Full GRACE Model**: story engine, predictor, and smart stimulus agent  

## 7.7. Daily Simulation Cycle
For each day:
1. environment generates events;
2. elder state changes;
3. social ties respond;
4. story engine generates heroic story;
5. GRACE Index is computed;
6. predictor estimates future risk;
7. stimulus agent may intervene; and
8. next-day state is updated.

## 7.8. Example Events
- bereavement reminder,
- pain flare or mobility issue,
- family silence,
- family visit,
- mentoring opportunity,
- spiritual holiday,
- missed transport, and
- community invitation.

## 7.9. Hypotheses
- **H1:** Story creation improves Narrative Continuity versus baseline.  
- **H2:** Fast prediction improves early detection of risk.  
- **H3:** Smart stimuli reduce time spent in low GRACE zones.  
- **H4:** Full GRACE model yields highest mean GRACE Index.  
- **H5:** Full model yields fastest recovery after adverse shocks.  
- **H6:** Hero’s-journey framing increases successful reframing of difficult days.  

## 7.10. Metrics
Primary outcomes:
- mean GRACE Index,
- percentage of days in stable/flourishing zones,
- percentage of days in vulnerable/disrupted zones,
- recovery time after shock, and
- sustained decline probability.

Secondary outcomes:
- mean Narrative Continuity,
- reframing success rate,
- social reconnection count,
- contribution activation rate, and
- intervention efficiency.

## 7.11. Expected Results
We expect:
- **Story Only** to improve meaning and narrative continuity,
- **Story + Prediction** to improve early warning, and
- **Full Model** to deliver highest stability, fewer crisis descents, and faster recovery.

---

# 8. Discussion
GRACE reframes elder-oriented intelligent systems from tools of monitoring to ecosystems of **authorship, meaning, and relational continuity**. The GRACE Index adds a way to measure whether a person still feels present in their own life. The three added innovations deepen this contribution.

First, **daily hero’s-journey story creation** gives structure to adversity and supports dignity through narrative reframing. Second, the **fast state predictor** introduces anticipatory intelligence, enabling action before significant decline. Third, the **smart stimulus agent** converts prediction into timely, personalized support.

Together, these features transform GRACE from a passive assessment framework into an **active narrative wellbeing ecology**. The proposed simulation experiment offers a controlled way to demonstrate these innovations before full deployment in real-world studies.

---

# 9. Implications

## 9.1. For HCI and AI
GRACE suggests moving:
- from profile to autobiography,
- from assistance to authorship,
- from reactive support to anticipatory support, and
- from single assistants to coordinated ecosystems (Jennings et al., 1998; Wooldridge, 2009; Shneiderman, 2020).

## 9.2. For Gerontology
The framework broadens wellbeing beyond symptoms to include:
- meaning,
- dignity,
- contribution,
- spiritual grounding, and
- narrative continuity (McAdams, 2001; Butler, 1963; Morrow-Howell et al., 2017).

## 9.3. For Community and Public Systems
GRACE suggests elder wellbeing infrastructures should support:
- relationship cultivation,
- contribution exchange,
- service access,
- community participation, and
- story and legacy preservation (Gurstein, 2007; Carroll, 2014).

---

# 10. Limitations and Future Work
This paper is conceptual and simulation-oriented. The GRACE Index weighting model, heroic integration model, and stimulus policies require empirical validation. Cultural adaptation will be essential, as narrative forms, spirituality, family structures, and notions of contribution vary. Future work should include:
- participatory co-design with older adults,
- prototype implementation,
- longitudinal trials,
- real-world evaluation of predictor performance, and
- ethical assessment of intervention boundaries.

---

# 11. Conclusion
GRACE offers a narrative-centered intelligent community computing ecosystem for elder wellbeing. It places the older adult at the center as continuing author and performer of life. The **GRACE Index** operationalizes daily narrative selfhood. The added innovations of **daily hero’s-journey story creation**, **AI-based fast state prediction**, and **smart stimulus intervention** make the ecosystem reflective, predictive, and self-stabilizing. The proposed **agent-based simulation experiment** provides a path for demonstrating these innovations and advancing future empirical work on humane AI for later life.

# GRACE: A Narrative-Centered Socio-Technical Ecosystem for Elder Wellbeing

## Abstract
Population aging is increasing demand for digital systems that support older adults’ wellbeing. Most existing systems emphasize safety, monitoring, and service coordination, but underrepresent identity, meaning, contribution, and relationship quality. This paper proposes **GRACE** (Growing, Resourceful, Active, and Enriched Life), a narrative-centered socio-technical ecosystem for elder wellbeing. GRACE is designed as an intelligent multiagent community computing framework that supports older adults in maintaining relationships with the spiritual self, family and friends, working colleagues, communities, and the wider world, including markets and public services. The paper makes four contributions. First, it introduces a narrative-centered theory of elder wellbeing grounded in relationship quality and contribution exchange. Second, it proposes the **GRACE Index**, a daily measure of whether a person remains “in the zone” of narrative selfhood during ordinary and difficult periods. Third, it extends the framework with three innovations: daily hero’s-journey story creation, an AI-based fast state predictor, and a smart stimulus agent for anticipatory support. Fourth, it presents an agent-based simulation design to evaluate these innovations. We argue that elder-oriented AI should move beyond deficit-oriented monitoring toward socio-technical ecosystems that preserve dignity, agency, reciprocity, and life authorship.

**Index Terms**—aging, wellbeing, narrative identity, multiagent systems, human-centered AI, socio-technical systems, community computing, eldercare technology.

---

# I. Introduction
Population aging is reshaping healthcare, labor, family structures, and public services. In response, digital systems for older adults have grown rapidly, especially in telehealth, remote monitoring, medication support, and care coordination. These systems are valuable, but many are built around narrow assumptions: that old age is primarily a condition of decline, risk, and dependency.

That assumption is socially and technically limiting. Older adults are not only patients or service users. They are also storytellers, mentors, workers, caregivers, citizens, spiritual beings, and bearers of memory and legacy. Their wellbeing depends not only on safety and functional support, but also on continuity of identity, meaningful relationships, opportunities to contribute, and the capacity to interpret life events within a coherent story.

This paper proposes **GRACE**—**Growing, Resourceful, Active, and Enriched Life**—as a socio-technical ecosystem for elder wellbeing. GRACE is an intelligent community computing framework organized around five relational domains: 1) spiritual self, 2) family and friends, 3) working colleagues, 4) communities, and 5) the wider world, including markets and public services. GRACE is grounded in the premise that elder wellbeing is relational and that the quality of relationships shapes the exchange of care, recognition, participation, and contribution.

The central design claim of GRACE is that **narrative identity** should be treated as a core systems concept. Older adults should not be modeled merely through static profiles, health states, or risk scores. They should be understood as persons living through unfolding stories. GRACE therefore acts as a **live autobiographical ecosystem**, helping elders remain connected to selfhood across daily life, transitions, and adversity.

To operationalize this vision, the paper introduces the **GRACE Index**, a daily measure of whether a person remains “in the zone” of narrative selfhood. The paper further adds three innovations: 1) a **Daily Story Creation Engine** that frames lived experience through the hero’s journey, 2) an **AI-based Fast State Predictor** that forecasts near-future wellbeing state, and 3) a **Smart Stimulus Agent** that initiates timely, personalized prompts to preserve wellbeing and authorship. Finally, the paper proposes an **agent-based simulation design** to demonstrate these innovations.

## Contributions
This paper makes four contributions:

1. **A socio-technical framework** for elder wellbeing centered on narrative identity, relationship quality, and contribution exchange.  
2. **The GRACE Index**, a daily metric of narrative selfhood and adaptive continuity.  
3. **Three AI-enabled innovations**—daily story creation, fast state prediction, and smart stimuli—for anticipatory wellbeing support.  
4. **An agent-based simulation design** for evaluating these mechanisms before real-world deployment.

---

# II. Related Work

## A. Aging Technologies and Deficit-Oriented Design
Technologies for older adults have often focused on safety, surveillance, and functional support (Sixsmith & Gutman, 2013; Czaja et al., 2006; Mitzner et al., 2010). While these systems can improve independence and care delivery, they may also reproduce deficit-oriented assumptions about aging. HCI scholarship has argued that such framings narrow the design space and underplay agency, participation, and dignity (Vines et al., 2015).

## B. Social Connectedness and Wellbeing
Social relationships are strongly associated with wellbeing, resilience, and mortality outcomes (Holt-Lunstad et al., 2010). Social isolation and perceived loneliness can negatively affect cognition and health (Cacioppo & Hawkley, 2009). In later life, relationship quality matters as much as contact frequency, including dimensions such as reciprocity, belonging, and social integration (Cornwell & Waite, 2009; Berkman et al., 2000).

## C. Narrative Identity and Later Life
Narrative identity theory suggests that individuals construct a sense of self through evolving life stories (McAdams, 2001; Habermas & Bluck, 2000; Singer, 2004). In later life, autobiographical continuity and life review become especially important for meaning-making, legacy, and coping with transition (Butler, 1963; Bohlmeijer et al., 2007). Yet most digital systems do not operationalize narrative identity as a core computational construct.

## D. Generativity and Productive Aging
Older adults continue to contribute through mentoring, caregiving, volunteering, advising, and cultural transmission. Generativity is a major source of meaning and self-worth (McAdams & de St. Aubin, 1992; Erikson, 1982). Productive aging research likewise emphasizes the value of creating roles and systems that support continued contribution (Morrow-Howell et al., 2017).

## E. Community Computing, Multiagent Systems, and Human-Centered AI
Community informatics has shown how digital infrastructures can support participation, local engagement, and social inclusion (Gurstein, 2007; Carroll, 2014). Multiagent systems offer a framework for distributed intelligence, coordination, and adaptive decision-making (Jennings et al., 1998; Wooldridge, 2009). Human-centered AI emphasizes reliability, safety, and alignment with human values (Shneiderman, 2020). GRACE integrates these strands into a single socio-technical architecture.

## F. Research Gap
Existing work rarely integrates all of the following in one coherent framework:
- narrative identity,
- relationship quality,
- contribution exchange,
- community participation,
- institutional and market access,
- predictive wellbeing support.

GRACE addresses this gap by proposing a narrative-centered, socially grounded AI ecosystem for elder wellbeing.

---

# III. GRACE as a Socio-Technical Ecosystem

## A. Core Premise
GRACE is based on a simple claim: elder wellbeing emerges from the interaction of **identity, relationships, contribution, and access**. A person flourishes when they remain connected to who they are, to people who matter, to ways of contributing, and to the broader systems that structure everyday life.

## B. Five Relational Domains
GRACE supports the elder’s relationship with:
1. **Spiritual self**  
2. **Family and friends**  
3. **Working colleagues**  
4. **Communities**  
5. **Wider world**, including markets and public services  

These domains define the social and institutional ecology of later life.

## C. Narrative Identity as System Core
Rather than using a conventional user profile, GRACE maintains a **Living Autobiography Model**. This includes:
- life chapters,
- major transitions,
- key roles,
- relationships,
- values and beliefs,
- strengths and contributions,
- memories,
- current concerns,
- future hopes.

This model helps the system interpret needs and opportunities in biographical context.

## D. Theater-of-Life Perspective
GRACE may be understood as a **theater of life**:
- the elder is the lead performer and author,
- life settings are stages,
- daily events are scenes,
- human and AI participants form the cast,
- narrative identity is the script in progress.

This metaphor is not decorative; it is a design principle. It emphasizes authorship, visibility, and continuity.

---

# IV. The GRACE Index

## A. Purpose
The **GRACE Index** is a daily composite indicator designed to assess whether a person remains “in the zone” of narrative selfhood. This means the person still experiences life as meaningfully their own, even when facing difficulty, grief, illness, or uncertainty.

The index is intended to measure **narrative integrity**, not merely mood or symptom burden.

## B. Dimensions
The index has six dimensions:
- **Narrative Continuity**
- **Spiritual Grounding**
- **Relational Vitality**
- **Contribution and Usefulness**
- **Community and World Engagement**
- **Adaptive Resilience**

## C. Scoring Model
Let
- \(N\) = Narrative Continuity
- \(S\) = Spiritual Grounding
- \(R\) = Relational Vitality
- \(C\) = Contribution and Usefulness
- \(E\) = Community and World Engagement
- \(A\) = Adaptive Resilience

Then the daily GRACE Index is:

\[
G_t = 0.22N_t + 0.14S_t + 0.20R_t + 0.16C_t + 0.13E_t + 0.15A_t
\]

with scores normalized to 0–100.

## D. Zone Interpretation
- **85–100**: Flourishing Zone  
- **70–84**: Stable Narrative Zone  
- **50–69**: Vulnerability Zone  
- **30–49**: Disruption Zone  
- **0–29**: Fragmentation Risk Zone  

The important feature is that a person may remain in a stable narrative zone even during distress if selfhood, belonging, and meaning remain intact.

---

# V. Three AI-Enabled Innovations

## A. Daily Story Creation Through the Hero’s Journey
GRACE includes a **Daily Story Creation Engine** that interprets everyday life through the hero’s journey. Instead of storing events as isolated entries, it reframes them through elements such as:
- ordinary world,
- call to adventure,
- trial,
- guide,
- courage,
- insight,
- return.

Let \(H_t \in [0,1]\) denote the daily heroic integration score:

\[
H_t = \alpha_1T_t + \alpha_2Gd_t + \alpha_3Co_t + \alpha_4In_t
\]

where \(T_t\), \(Gd_t\), \(Co_t\), and \(In_t\) represent trial recognition, presence of guidance, courageous response, and insight gained.

This mechanism is intended to buffer narrative fragmentation by preserving meaning under adversity.

## B. AI-Based Fast State Predictor
GRACE includes a **Fast State Predictor** that forecasts near-future wellbeing state using recent GRACE Index trajectories, event patterns, and story features.

\[
\hat{G}_{t+\tau} = P(G_{t-\ell:t}, X_{t-\ell:t}, H_{t-\ell:t}, U_{t-\ell:t})
\]

where:
- \(P\) is the predictor,
- \(X\) is the vector of GRACE dimensions,
- \(H\) is the heroic integration score,
- \(U\) represents contextual events,
- \(\ell\) is the lookback window,
- \(\tau\) is the prediction horizon.

This predictor enables the system to shift from reactive monitoring to anticipatory support.

## C. Smart Stimulus Agent
GRACE also includes a **Smart Stimulus Agent** that selects a personalized prompt or support action based on predicted state.

\[
a_t \in \mathcal{A} = \{a^{nar}, a^{rel}, a^{spi}, a^{con}, a^{com}, a^{prac}, a^{none}\}
\]

where action types correspond to narrative, relational, spiritual, contribution, community, practical, or no intervention.

Policy:

\[
a_t = \pi(\hat{Z}_{t+1}, X_t, p)
\]

where \(\hat{Z}_{t+1}\) is the predicted zone and \(p\) is the person’s profile and preferences.

The aim is not behavior control, but supportive intervention that helps preserve dignity, continuity, and agency.

---

# VI. Multiagent Architecture and Governance

## A. Core Agents
The GRACE ecosystem includes:
- Narrative Agent
- Relationship Agent
- Wellbeing Agent
- Spiritual Companion Agent
- Family Coordination Agent
- Work and Contribution Agent
- Community Connector Agent
- Service Access Agent
- Memory and Legacy Agent
- Ethics and Safety Agent

## B. Shared Data Core
These agents interact through shared structures:
- Living Autobiography Model
- Relationship Graph
- Contribution Ledger
- GRACE Index Engine
- Hero’s Journey Story Store
- Prediction Model Store
- Consent and Trust Manager
- Event Timeline

## C. Closed-Loop Operation
The system follows a closed loop:
1. daily events occur,
2. story interpretation is generated,
3. GRACE Index is updated,
4. state prediction is computed,
5. stimulus action is selected,
6. outcome feeds back into the next day’s state.

## D. Governance and Ethical Safeguards
Because GRACE handles intimate autobiographical and relational data, governance is central. The system should include:
- consent-based data access,
- explainable interventions,
- adjustable autonomy,
- human override,
- privacy protection,
- safeguards against manipulative prompting.

These safeguards are critical for aligning predictive systems with dignity and agency.

---

# VII. Agent-Based Simulation Design

## A. Objective
Before real-world deployment, the three innovations should be evaluated in simulation. The goal is to test whether story creation, prediction, and smart stimuli improve narrative stability under varying life conditions.

## B. Agents
The simulation includes:
- **Elder Agent**
- **Narrative Agent**
- **Predictor Agent**
- **Stimulus Agent**
- **Social Tie Agents**
- **Environment Agent**

## C. State Representation
For person \(i\) on day \(t\):

\[
X_{i,t} = [N_{i,t}, S_{i,t}, R_{i,t}, C_{i,t}, E_{i,t}, A_{i,t}]
\]

and

\[
G_{i,t} = 0.22N_{i,t} + 0.14S_{i,t} + 0.20R_{i,t} + 0.16C_{i,t} + 0.13E_{i,t} + 0.15A_{i,t}
\]

State transition:

\[
X_{i,t+1} = f(X_{i,t}, U_{i,t}, H_{i,t}, A_{i,t}^{stim}, \epsilon_{i,t})
\]

## D. Experimental Conditions
1. **Baseline**: monitoring only  
2. **Story Only**: daily story creation active  
3. **Story + Prediction**: story creation plus fast prediction  
4. **Full GRACE**: story creation, prediction, and smart stimuli  

## E. Hypotheses
- **H1**: Story creation increases Narrative Continuity.  
- **H2**: Fast prediction improves early detection of decline.  
- **H3**: Smart stimuli reduce time spent in low GRACE zones.  
- **H4**: Full GRACE yields the highest average GRACE Index.  
- **H5**: Full GRACE reduces recovery time after negative shocks.  

## F. Outcome Metrics
Primary outcomes:
- mean GRACE Index,
- percentage of stable-zone days,
- percentage of vulnerable/disrupted days,
- recovery time after shocks,
- sustained decline probability.

Secondary outcomes:
- narrative continuity during adversity,
- reframing success,
- reconnection events,
- contribution activation,
- intervention efficiency.

---

# VIII. Discussion
GRACE contributes a socio-technical reframing of elder-oriented AI. Instead of centering decline, it centers authorship. Instead of treating wellbeing as only a clinical or behavioral state, it treats wellbeing as **narrative, relational, and participatory**.

For IEEE audiences, the significance is not just a new application domain, but a broader systems argument: intelligent technologies for vulnerable populations should be evaluated not only by accuracy or efficiency, but by whether they preserve human agency, dignity, and continuity of self. The GRACE Index, prediction loop, and stimulus agent together illustrate how computational systems might be built around those values.

The proposed simulation design is important because it allows evaluation of predictive and intervention policies before deployment in sensitive populations. It also creates a testbed for governance questions, such as when intervention becomes intrusive, how predictive uncertainty should be handled, and how consent constraints affect system behavior.

---

# IX. Limitations and Future Work
This paper is conceptual and simulation-oriented. The GRACE Index weights, story integration parameters, and intervention policies remain provisional. Empirical validation with older adults is still needed. Cultural variation in narrative forms, family relations, spirituality, and contribution norms will also require adaptation.

Future work should include:
- participatory co-design,
- prototype implementation,
- simulation results,
- pilot deployment,
- predictive model validation,
- governance and fairness analysis.

---

# X. Conclusion
GRACE proposes a narrative-centered socio-technical ecosystem for elder wellbeing. By integrating relationship quality, autobiographical continuity, predictive modeling, and anticipatory intervention, it offers an alternative to deficit-oriented aging technologies. The framework suggests that intelligent systems for older adults should be designed not only to manage risk, but to sustain dignity, reciprocity, and the ongoing authorship of life.

## VIII-A. Technology and Society Implications

GRACE has implications beyond eldercare technology. It raises broader questions about how intelligent systems should be designed for populations whose wellbeing depends not only on functional support, but also on dignity, identity, and social recognition. In this sense, GRACE is not merely a service platform; it is a model of how AI systems can participate in socially sensitive human environments.

### A. From Monitoring to Meaning
Many current systems for older adults are optimized for surveillance, compliance, and risk reduction. While these goals are important, they can narrow the role of technology to observation and control. GRACE suggests a different direction: intelligent systems should also support meaning-making, narrative continuity, and social participation. This shift matters because technologies do not merely respond to social realities; they also shape how people are seen. Systems that treat older adults mainly as risks may reinforce dependency narratives. Systems that recognize them as authors and contributors may strengthen agency and visibility.

### B. The Social Construction of Aging Through Technology
Technologies embody assumptions about aging. A deficit-oriented system can indirectly reproduce ageism by centering decline, incapacity, and burden. GRACE instead frames later life as a stage of ongoing story, contribution, and relational development. This reframing is socially consequential. It suggests that socio-technical systems should not simply adapt to older adults, but should also challenge reductive models of aging embedded in digital infrastructures.

### C. Narrative Data as a New Category of Sensitive Data
GRACE relies not only on behavioral or health data, but also on autobiographical, relational, and interpretive data. Such data are particularly sensitive because they concern identity, memory, meaning, and social bonds. This raises governance issues beyond conventional privacy protection. Questions include:
- Who owns a person’s life story representation?
- Who may view, edit, or interpret autobiographical records?
- How should conflicting family narratives be handled?
- When does story assistance become narrative imposition?

These questions suggest the need for stronger governance models for narrative data in AI systems.

### D. Prediction, Intervention, and the Risk of Paternalism
The Fast State Predictor and Smart Stimulus Agent introduce anticipatory action, but predictive support can become paternalistic if it overrides autonomy or subtly steers behavior without sufficient transparency. In elder-oriented systems, this risk is especially important because users may already be positioned socially as vulnerable or dependent. GRACE therefore implies that predictive interventions should be:
- explainable,
- consent-aware,
- adjustable in intensity,
- reversible,
- subject to human override.

The ethical aim is to assist without taking over authorship.

### E. Human-Centered AI in Socially Embedded Contexts
GRACE contributes to human-centered AI by suggesting that reliability and safety are necessary but not sufficient. In socially embedded contexts, systems should also be evaluated by whether they preserve:
- agency,
- dignity,
- reciprocity,
- self-interpretation,
- social belonging.

This implies a broader evaluation framework for AI systems in society: not only performance metrics, but also effects on identity and relationships.

### F. Social Infrastructure and Inclusion
GRACE also highlights that elder wellbeing is shaped by access to communities, markets, institutions, and public services. Intelligent systems for later life should therefore not be designed only as personal assistants, but as connectors to social infrastructure. This has policy implications. Public-interest technologies for aging should support participation, not just care; contribution, not just dependency; and inclusion, not just monitoring.

### G. Implications for Technology Governance
From a technology-and-society perspective, GRACE suggests five governance principles for elder-oriented AI:
1. **Narrative sovereignty**: the elder retains primary authority over their life story representation.
2. **Consentful prediction**: predictive analytics must be understandable and optional.
3. **Non-manipulative intervention**: prompts should support, not coerce.
4. **Relational accountability**: systems must account for effects on family, community, and institutional relationships.
5. **Dignity-centered evaluation**: success should include preservation of selfhood, not only efficiency or compliance.

### H. Broader Relevance
Although GRACE is designed for older adults, its implications extend to other domains where human wellbeing depends on identity continuity and social participation, including disability support, chronic illness management, rehabilitation, and mental health recovery. As AI systems move further into everyday life, the GRACE framework suggests a broader principle: intelligent socio-technical systems should help people remain present in their own lives.

# GRACE: A Narrative-Centered Socio-Technical Ecosystem for Elder Wellbeing

## Abstract
Population aging is increasing demand for digital systems that support older adults’ wellbeing. Most existing systems emphasize safety, monitoring, and service coordination, but underrepresent identity, meaning, contribution, and relationship quality. This paper proposes **GRACE** (Growing, Resourceful, Active, and Enriched Life), a narrative-centered socio-technical ecosystem for elder wellbeing. GRACE is designed as an intelligent multiagent community computing framework that supports older adults in maintaining relationships with the spiritual self, family and friends, working colleagues, communities, and the wider world, including markets and public services. The paper makes four contributions. First, it introduces a narrative-centered theory of elder wellbeing grounded in relationship quality and contribution exchange. Second, it proposes the **GRACE Index**, a daily measure of whether a person remains “in the zone” of narrative selfhood during ordinary and difficult periods. Third, it extends the framework with three innovations: daily hero’s-journey story creation, an AI-based fast state predictor, and a smart stimulus agent for anticipatory support. Fourth, it presents an agent-based simulation design to evaluate these innovations. We argue that elder-oriented AI should move beyond deficit-oriented monitoring toward socio-technical ecosystems that preserve dignity, agency, reciprocity, and life authorship.

**Index Terms**—aging, wellbeing, narrative identity, multiagent systems, human-centered AI, socio-technical systems, community computing, eldercare technology.

---

# I. Introduction
Population aging is reshaping healthcare, labor, family structures, and public services. In response, digital systems for older adults have grown rapidly, especially in telehealth, remote monitoring, medication support, and care coordination. These systems are valuable, but many are built around narrow assumptions: that old age is primarily a condition of decline, risk, and dependency.

That assumption is socially and technically limiting. Older adults are not only patients or service users. They are also storytellers, mentors, workers, caregivers, citizens, spiritual beings, and bearers of memory and legacy. Their wellbeing depends not only on safety and functional support, but also on continuity of identity, meaningful relationships, opportunities to contribute, and the capacity to interpret life events within a coherent story.

This paper proposes **GRACE**—**Growing, Resourceful, Active, and Enriched Life**—as a socio-technical ecosystem for elder wellbeing. GRACE is an intelligent community computing framework organized around five relational domains: 1) spiritual self, 2) family and friends, 3) working colleagues, 4) communities, and 5) the wider world, including markets and public services. GRACE is grounded in the premise that elder wellbeing is relational and that the quality of relationships shapes the exchange of care, recognition, participation, and contribution.

The central design claim of GRACE is that **narrative identity** should be treated as a core systems concept. Older adults should not be modeled merely through static profiles, health states, or risk scores. They should be understood as persons living through unfolding stories. GRACE therefore acts as a **live autobiographical ecosystem**, helping elders remain connected to selfhood across daily life, transitions, and adversity.

To operationalize this vision, the paper introduces the **GRACE Index**, a daily measure of whether a person remains “in the zone” of narrative selfhood. The paper further adds three innovations: 1) a **Daily Story Creation Engine** that frames lived experience through the hero’s journey, 2) an **AI-based Fast State Predictor** that forecasts near-future wellbeing state, and 3) a **Smart Stimulus Agent** that initiates timely, personalized prompts to preserve wellbeing and authorship. Finally, the paper proposes an **agent-based simulation design** to demonstrate these innovations.

## Contributions
This paper makes four contributions:

1. **A socio-technical framework** for elder wellbeing centered on narrative identity, relationship quality, and contribution exchange.  
2. **The GRACE Index**, a daily metric of narrative selfhood and adaptive continuity.  
3. **Three AI-enabled innovations**—daily story creation, fast state prediction, and smart stimuli—for anticipatory wellbeing support.  
4. **An agent-based simulation design** for evaluating these mechanisms before real-world deployment.

---

# II. Related Work

## A. Aging Technologies and Deficit-Oriented Design
Technologies for older adults have often focused on safety, surveillance, and functional support (Sixsmith & Gutman, 2013; Czaja et al., 2006; Mitzner et al., 2010). While these systems can improve independence and care delivery, they may also reproduce deficit-oriented assumptions about aging. HCI scholarship has argued that such framings narrow the design space and underplay agency, participation, and dignity (Vines et al., 2015).

## B. Social Connectedness and Wellbeing
Social relationships are strongly associated with wellbeing, resilience, and mortality outcomes (Holt-Lunstad et al., 2010). Social isolation and perceived loneliness can negatively affect cognition and health (Cacioppo & Hawkley, 2009). In later life, relationship quality matters as much as contact frequency, including dimensions such as reciprocity, belonging, and social integration (Cornwell & Waite, 2009; Berkman et al., 2000).

## C. Narrative Identity and Later Life
Narrative identity theory suggests that individuals construct a sense of self through evolving life stories (McAdams, 2001; Habermas & Bluck, 2000; Singer, 2004). In later life, autobiographical continuity and life review become especially important for meaning-making, legacy, and coping with transition (Butler, 1963; Bohlmeijer et al., 2007). Yet most digital systems do not operationalize narrative identity as a core computational construct.

## D. Generativity and Productive Aging
Older adults continue to contribute through mentoring, caregiving, volunteering, advising, and cultural transmission. Generativity is a major source of meaning and self-worth (McAdams & de St. Aubin, 1992; Erikson, 1982). Productive aging research likewise emphasizes the value of creating roles and systems that support continued contribution (Morrow-Howell et al., 2017).

## E. Community Computing, Multiagent Systems, and Human-Centered AI
Community informatics has shown how digital infrastructures can support participation, local engagement, and social inclusion (Gurstein, 2007; Carroll, 2014). Multiagent systems offer a framework for distributed intelligence, coordination, and adaptive decision-making (Jennings et al., 1998; Wooldridge, 2009). Human-centered AI emphasizes reliability, safety, and alignment with human values (Shneiderman, 2020). GRACE integrates these strands into a single socio-technical architecture.

## F. Research Gap
Existing work rarely integrates all of the following in one coherent framework:
- narrative identity,
- relationship quality,
- contribution exchange,
- community participation,
- institutional and market access,
- predictive wellbeing support.

GRACE addresses this gap by proposing a narrative-centered, socially grounded AI ecosystem for elder wellbeing.

---

4. **Full GRACE**: story creation, prediction, and smart stimuli  

## E. Hypotheses
- **H1**: Story creation increases Narrative Continuity.  
- **H2**: Fast prediction improves early detection of decline.  
- **H3**: Smart stimuli reduce time spent in low GRACE zones.  
- **H4**: Full GRACE yields the highest average GRACE Index.  
- **H5**: Full GRACE reduces recovery time after negative shocks.  

## F. Outcome Metrics
Primary outcomes:
- mean GRACE Index,
- percentage of stable-zone days,
- percentage of vulnerable/disrupted days,
- recovery time after shocks,
- sustained decline probability.

Secondary outcomes:
- narrative continuity during adversity,
- reframing success,
- reconnection events,
- contribution activation,
- intervention efficiency.

---

# VIII. Technology and Society Implications

## A. From Monitoring to Meaning
Many current systems for older adults are optimized for surveillance, compliance, and risk reduction. While these goals are important, they can narrow the role of technology to observation and control. GRACE suggests a different direction: intelligent systems should also support meaning-making, narrative continuity, and social participation. This shift matters because technologies do not merely respond to social realities; they also shape how people are seen. Systems that treat older adults mainly as risks may reinforce dependency narratives. Systems that recognize them as authors and contributors may strengthen agency and visibility.

## B. The Social Construction of Aging Through Technology
Technologies embody assumptions about aging. A deficit-oriented system can indirectly reproduce ageism by centering decline, incapacity, and burden. GRACE instead frames later life as a stage of ongoing story, contribution, and relational development. This reframing is socially consequential. It suggests that socio-technical systems should not simply adapt to older adults, but should also challenge reductive models of aging embedded in digital infrastructures.

## C. Narrative Data as a New Category of Sensitive Data
GRACE relies not only on behavioral or health data, but also on autobiographical, relational, and interpretive data. Such data are particularly sensitive because they concern identity, memory, meaning, and social bonds. This raises governance issues beyond conventional privacy protection. Questions include:
- Who owns a person’s life story representation?
- Who may view, edit, or interpret autobiographical records?
- How should conflicting family narratives be handled?
- When does story assistance become narrative imposition?

These questions suggest the need for stronger governance models for narrative data in AI systems.

## D. Prediction, Intervention, and the Risk of Paternalism
The Fast State Predictor and Smart Stimulus Agent introduce anticipatory action, but predictive support can become paternalistic if it overrides autonomy or subtly steers behavior without sufficient transparency. In elder-oriented systems, this risk is especially important because users may already be positioned socially as vulnerable or dependent. GRACE therefore implies that predictive interventions should be:
- explainable,
- consent-aware,
- adjustable in intensity,
- reversible,
- subject to human override.

The ethical aim is to assist without taking over authorship.

## E. Human-Centered AI in Socially Embedded Contexts
GRACE contributes to human-centered AI by suggesting that reliability and safety are necessary but not sufficient. In socially embedded contexts, systems should also be evaluated by whether they preserve:
- agency,
- dignity,
- reciprocity,
- self-interpretation,
- social belonging.

This implies a broader evaluation framework for AI systems in society: not only performance metrics, but also effects on identity and relationships.

## F. Social Infrastructure and Inclusion
GRACE also highlights that elder wellbeing is shaped by access to communities, markets, institutions, and public services. Intelligent systems for later life should therefore not be designed only as personal assistants, but as connectors to social infrastructure. This has policy implications. Public-interest technologies for aging should support participation, not just care; contribution, not just dependency; and inclusion, not just monitoring.

## G. Implications for Technology Governance
From a technology-and-society perspective, GRACE suggests five governance principles for elder-oriented AI:
1. **Narrative sovereignty**: the elder retains primary authority over their life story representation.  
2. **Consentful prediction**: predictive analytics must be understandable and optional.  
3. **Non-manipulative intervention**: prompts should support, not coerce.  
4. **Relational accountability**: systems must account for effects on family, community, and institutional relationships.  
5. **Dignity-centered evaluation**: success should include preservation of selfhood, not only efficiency or compliance.  

## H. Broader Relevance
Although GRACE is designed for older adults, its implications extend to other domains where human wellbeing depends on identity continuity and social participation, including disability support, chronic illness management, rehabilitation, and mental health recovery. As AI systems move further into everyday life, the GRACE framework suggests a broader principle: intelligent socio-technical systems should help people remain present in their own lives.

---

# IX. Discussion
GRACE contributes a socio-technical reframing of elder-oriented AI. Instead of centering decline, it centers authorship. Instead of treating wellbeing as only a clinical or behavioral state, it treats wellbeing as **narrative, relational, and participatory**.

For IEEE audiences, the significance is not just a new application domain, but a broader systems argument: intelligent technologies for vulnerable populations should be evaluated not only by accuracy or efficiency, but by whether they preserve human agency, dignity, and continuity of self. The GRACE Index, prediction loop, and stimulus agent together illustrate how computational systems might be built around those values.

The proposed simulation design is important because it allows evaluation of predictive and intervention policies before deployment in sensitive populations. It also creates a testbed for governance questions, such as when intervention becomes intrusive, how predictive uncertainty should be handled, and how consent constraints affect system behavior.

---

# X. Limitations and Future Work
This paper is conceptual and simulation-oriented. The GRACE Index weights, story integration parameters, and intervention policies remain provisional. Empirical validation with older adults is still needed. Cultural variation in narrative forms, family relations, spirituality, and contribution norms will also require adaptation.

Future work should include:
- participatory co-design,
- prototype implementation,
- simulation results,
- pilot deployment,
- predictive model validation,
- governance and fairness analysis.

---

# XI. Conclusion
GRACE proposes a narrative-centered socio-technical ecosystem for elder wellbeing. By integrating relationship quality, autobiographical continuity, predictive modeling, and anticipatory intervention, it offers an alternative to deficit-oriented aging technologies. The framework suggests that intelligent systems for older adults should be designed not only to manage risk, but to sustain dignity, reciprocity, and the ongoing authorship of life.

## Add to **Section I. Introduction**
A high-level overview of the GRACE ecosystem is shown in **Fig. 1**, which situates the elder within five relational domains and places narrative identity at the center of system design. **Fig. 2** presents the multiagent architecture, while **Fig. 3** summarizes the closed-loop wellbeing cycle linking story creation, daily assessment, prediction, and intervention. The principal constructs used throughout the framework are summarized in **Tables I–V**.

---

## Add to **Section III. GRACE as a Socio-Technical Ecosystem**
The overall socio-technical structure of GRACE is illustrated in **Fig. 1**. As shown there, the system is organized around five relational domains that together define the elder’s social and institutional ecology. These domains, together with their associated wellbeing functions and example system supports, are summarized in **Table I**.

The biographical core of the system is the **Living Autobiography Model**, whose main constructs are summarized in **Table II**. Rather than serving as a static profile, this model allows GRACE to interpret events, needs, and opportunities in autobiographical context.

---

## Add to **Section IV. The GRACE Index**
The six dimensions of the GRACE Index, along with example indicators and data sources, are summarized in **Table III**. The proposed weighting structure and score-zone interpretation are presented in **Table IV**. A visual summary of the six dimensions and five interpretive zones is shown in **Fig. 5**.

This representation is important because the GRACE Index is intended to distinguish temporary distress from deeper narrative fragmentation. As shown in **Fig. 5** and **Table IV**, the model allows a person to remain in a stable narrative zone even during adversity if continuity of self, meaning, and belonging remain intact.

---

## Add to **Section V. Three AI-Enabled Innovations**
The three AI-enabled innovations introduced in this paper are summarized in **Table V**. Together, these mechanisms transform GRACE from a descriptive monitoring framework into a predictive and supportive socio-technical ecosystem.

The first innovation, daily story creation through the hero’s journey, is illustrated in **Fig. 4**. The figure shows how ordinary daily events may be interpreted through narrative elements such as call, trial, guide, courage, insight, and return. This design allows adversity to be processed as meaningful struggle rather than fragmented disruption.

The second and third innovations—the Fast State Predictor and Smart Stimulus Agent—operate within the closed-loop mechanism shown in **Fig. 3**, where daily experience is interpreted, assessed, forecast, and responded to through personalized support.

---

The full GRACE multiagent architecture is shown in **Fig. 2**, and the roles of the principal agents are summarized in **Table VI**. As shown there, the architecture combines specialized agents with a shared data core that includes the Living Autobiography Model, relationship graph, contribution ledger, GRACE Index engine, and prediction store.

The governance implications of this architecture are significant. **Table VII** identifies key risks, including paternalism, narrative imposition, privacy breaches, over-intervention, and prediction opacity, together with corresponding safeguards. These governance concerns are summarized visually in **Fig. 7**, which presents five design principles: narrative sovereignty, consentful prediction, non-manipulative intervention, relational accountability, and dignity-centered evaluation.

---

The proposed simulation structure is shown in **Fig. 6**, which depicts the interactions among the Elder Agent, Narrative Agent, Predictor Agent, Stimulus Agent, Social Tie Agents, and Environment Agent. The experimental conditions and hypotheses are summarized in **Table VIII**, while the principal state variables and outcome metrics are presented in **Table IX**. Together, **Fig. 6** and **Tables VIII–IX** provide a formal basis for evaluating whether story creation, prediction, and smart stimuli improve narrative stability and recovery under adverse conditions.

---

## Add to **Section VIII. Technology and Society Implications**
The governance framework presented in **Fig. 7** and **Table VII** clarifies that the technological significance of GRACE lies not only in predictive capability, but also in how prediction and intervention are socially constrained. In this sense, the framework argues that intelligent systems for older adults should be assessed not solely by performance, but also by their effects on dignity, agency, identity, and relational life.

The figures establish the conceptual, architectural, dynamic, and governance logic of the framework, while the tables make explicit its constructs, indicators, risks, and evaluation strategy.

| Family and Friends | Intimate and enduring personal relationships | belonging, affection, care continuity, emotional security, shared memory | family coordination, message prompts, memory sharing, visit planning |

## Table II. Core Constructs of the Living Autobiography Model

**Caption:**  
**Table II.** Core constructs of the Living Autobiography Model and their roles in personalization and interpretation within the GRACE ecosystem.

| Construct | Description | Example Data Elements | Role in Personalization |
|---|---|---|---|
| Life Chapters | Major periods or phases of life | childhood, career phase, retirement, caregiving years | supports contextual interpretation of current events |
| Major Transitions | Turning points and disruptions | bereavement, migration, illness onset, retirement | supports sensitivity to recurring vulnerabilities and resilience patterns |
| Key Relationships | Important persons and social ties | spouse, children, friends, mentor, colleague | informs relational prompts and support priorities |
| Roles and Identities | Enduring self-definitions | parent, teacher, engineer, volunteer, believer | supports role continuity and contribution opportunities |
| Values and Beliefs | Moral, spiritual, and existential commitments | faith, service, independence, compassion | aligns prompts and interventions with personal meaning |
| Strengths and Skills | Capabilities and resources | mentoring, storytelling, teaching, organizing | supports contribution matching |
| Meaningful Places | Places of emotional or narrative significance | family home, workplace, temple, neighborhood park | supports memory prompts and place-based engagement |
| Rituals and Traditions | Recurrent meaningful practices | weekly worship, family dinners, anniversaries | supports temporal and spiritual grounding |
| Significant Memories | Events with autobiographical weight | marriage, awards, losses, milestones | supports story creation and identity continuity |
| Present Concerns | Current needs and stresses | loneliness, mobility limits, family conflict | supports adaptive intervention |
| Future Hopes | Forward-looking aims | legacy project, reconnecting with family, community service | supports future-oriented narrative continuity |

## Table III. GRACE Index Dimensions, Indicators, and Example Data Sources

**Caption:**  
**Table III.** Dimensions of the GRACE Index, their definitions, representative daily indicators, and example data sources.

| Dimension | Definition | Example Daily Indicators | Example Data Sources |
|---|---|---|---|
| Narrative Continuity | Sense of remaining connected to one’s life story and selfhood | felt like self, could make sense of the day, role continuity, future orientation | self-report, voice reflections, story engine outputs |
| Spiritual Grounding | Degree of inner peace, reflection, faith, or value alignment | prayer/reflection, gratitude, acceptance, values-based action | self-report, ritual logs, spiritual prompts |
| Relational Vitality | Quality of meaningful connection and belonging | meaningful contact, warmth of interaction, reciprocity, felt belonging | interaction logs, visit records, self-report |
| Contribution and Usefulness | Sense of helping, guiding, teaching, or being needed | mentoring, helping, advice-giving, feeling useful | contribution logs, task records, self-report |
| Community and World Engagement | Participation beyond the immediate self or household | event attendance, service access, neighborhood interaction, civic participation | activity logs, appointments, community records |
| Adaptive Resilience | Capacity to remain centered and recover during stress | coping, help-seeking, recovery after setbacks, staying grounded | self-report, predictor features, behavior trends |

---

## Table IV. GRACE Index Scoring Model and Zone Interpretation

**Caption:**  
**Table IV.** Proposed weighting model for the GRACE Index and interpretation of score zones.

### A. Dimension Weights

| Dimension | Symbol | Weight |
|---|---:|---:|
| Narrative Continuity | \(N\) | 0.22 |
| Spiritual Grounding | \(S\) | 0.14 |
| Relational Vitality | \(R\) | 0.20 |
| Contribution and Usefulness | \(C\) | 0.16 |
| Community and World Engagement | \(E\) | 0.13 |
| Adaptive Resilience | \(A\) | 0.15 |

### B. Zone Interpretation

| Zone | Score Range | Interpretation | Typical System Response |
|---|---:|---|---|
| Flourishing Zone | 85–100 | strong continuity, connection, contribution, and resilience | reinforce growth and legacy opportunities |
| Stable Narrative Zone | 70–84 | stable selfhood and functioning despite ordinary challenges | maintain routines and meaningful engagement |
| Vulnerability Zone | 50–69 | early signs of drift, withdrawal, or reduced engagement | initiate gentle, targeted support |
| Disruption Zone | 30–49 | significant weakening of narrative continuity or relational vitality | escalate coordinated intervention |
| Fragmentation Risk Zone | 0–29 | severe disconnection, overwhelm, or loss of self-authorship | urgent, high-touch support with human oversight |

---

## Table V. Three AI-Enabled Innovations in GRACE

**Caption:**  
**Table V.** Three AI-enabled innovations proposed in GRACE, including their inputs, functions, outputs, and expected benefits.

| Innovation | Primary Inputs | Core Function | Outputs | Expected Benefit |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Daily Story Creation Engine | daily events, emotional signals, interactions, reflections | converts daily life into hero’s-journey narrative structure | daily story, heroic integration score, narrative tags | improved meaning-making and narrative continuity |
| Fast State Predictor | GRACE Index history, trends, event context, story features | forecasts near-future wellbeing state and risk | predicted score, predicted zone, intervention urgency | earlier detection of decline |
| Smart Stimulus Agent | predicted state, preferences, consent rules, intervention history | selects personalized prompt or support action | narrative/relational/spiritual/contribution/community/practical stimulus | reduced time in vulnerable or disrupted states |

---

## Table VI. GRACE Agent Roles and Responsibilities

**Caption:**  
**Table VI.** Principal agents in the GRACE ecosystem, their responsibilities, major inputs, and outputs.

| Agent | Primary Responsibility | Main Inputs | Main Outputs | Related Domains |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Narrative Agent | maintain autobiographical continuity | reflections, memories, daily events | stories, narrative summaries, continuity signals | all domains |
| Relationship Agent | track and strengthen social ties | interaction logs, visit patterns | tie-strength estimates, reconnection suggestions | family, colleagues, communities |
| Wellbeing Agent | synthesize cross-domain wellbeing | GRACE dimensions, trends, events | state summaries, risk signals | all domains |
| Spiritual Companion Agent | support reflection and spiritual grounding | rituals, reflections, values | spiritual prompts, grounding support | spiritual self |
| Family Coordination Agent | coordinate communication and care continuity | family contacts, calendars, events | visit plans, updates, reminders | family and friends |
| Work and Contribution Agent | identify contribution opportunities | skills, roles, requests | mentoring matches, contribution prompts | colleagues, communities |
| Community Connector Agent | support participation and local belonging | local events, memberships, opportunities | engagement recommendations | communities |
| Service Access Agent | connect to services and institutions | appointments, transport, benefits needs | service guidance, access support | wider world |
| Memory and Legacy Agent | preserve long-term story and artifacts | stories, photos, milestones | archives, legacy packages | family, communities |
| Ethics and Safety Agent | enforce safeguards and accountability | consent rules, system actions, risk states | approvals, constraints, audit records | all domains |

---

## Table VII. Governance Risks and Safeguards

**Caption:**  
**Table VII.** Key governance risks in GRACE and representative safeguards for protecting dignity, autonomy, and relational integrity.

| Risk | Example | Potential Harm | Safeguard |
|---|---|---|---|
| Paternalism | system over-directs behavior | erosion of autonomy | adjustable autonomy, opt-out controls, human override |
| Narrative Imposition | system interprets life story too forcefully | distortion of selfhood | user-editable stories, narrative sovereignty, transparency |
| Privacy Breach | sensitive autobiographical data exposed | loss of trust, harm to dignity | access controls, encryption, consent-based sharing |
| Over-Intervention | too many prompts or alerts | fatigue, frustration, disengagement | rate limits, preference learning, intervention thresholds |
| Family Misuse | relatives use system to pressure elder | coercion or conflict | granular permissions, elder-first governance |
| Prediction Opacity | unclear reason for risk prediction | mistrust, confusion | explainable prediction summaries |
| Bias in Prediction | model underperforms for some groups | inequitable support | fairness auditing, subgroup validation |
| Emotional Harm | prompts surface painful memories badly | distress or retraumatization | sensitivity rules, contextual timing, human escalation |
| Dependency on System | overreliance on AI mediation | reduced direct human engagement | human-in-the-loop design, connection-first policies |

---

## Table VIII. Agent-Based Simulation Conditions and Hypotheses

**Caption:**  
**Table VIII.** Experimental conditions and hypotheses for the proposed agent-based simulation.

### A. Conditions

| Condition | Story Creation | Prediction | Stimulus Agent | Expected Effect |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Baseline | No | No | No | monitoring only |
| Story Only | Yes | No | No | improved narrative continuity |
| Story + Prediction | Yes | Yes | No | improved early risk detection |
| Full GRACE | Yes | Yes | Yes | highest stability and fastest recovery |

### B. Hypotheses

| Hypothesis | Statement |
|---|---|
| H1 | Story creation increases Narrative Continuity relative to baseline |
| H2 | Fast prediction improves early detection of decline |
| H3 | Smart stimuli reduce time spent in low GRACE zones |
| H4 | Full GRACE yields highest average GRACE Index |
| H5 | Full GRACE reduces recovery time after adverse shocks |

---

## Table IX. Simulation Variables and Outcome Metrics

**Caption:**  
**Table IX.** Principal state variables and outcome metrics used in the GRACE agent-based simulation.

| Variable / Metric | Definition | Type | Role in Analysis |
|---|---|---|---|
| \(N\) | Narrative Continuity | state variable | tracks self-story coherence |
| \(S\) | Spiritual Grounding | state variable | tracks inner steadiness |
| \(R\) | Relational Vitality | state variable | tracks connection quality |
| \(C\) | Contribution and Usefulness | state variable | tracks perceived usefulness |
| \(E\) | Community and World Engagement | state variable | tracks external participation |
| \(A\) | Adaptive Resilience | state variable | tracks coping and recovery |
| \(G\) | GRACE Index | composite metric | primary wellbeing outcome |
| \(H\) | Heroic Integration Score | derived metric | measures successful narrative reframing |
| Stable-Zone Days | proportion of days with \(G \geq 70\) | outcome metric | assesses sustained wellbeing |
| Vulnerable/Disrupted Days | proportion of days with \(G < 70\) | outcome metric | assesses instability |
| Recovery Time | days to return to stable zone after shock | outcome metric | assesses resilience |
| Reframing Success | proportion of adverse days with high \(H\) | outcome metric | assesses story engine effect |
| Intervention Efficiency | gain in \(G\) per stimulus action | outcome metric | assesses stimulus value |
